                                                 Q IAN QI                                        3

5                                                                                                5
                    This paper develops Neural Bellman Operators for policy evaluation
6                                                                                                6
                 and feasible improvement in controlled economies. Centered continuation
7                                                                                                7
                 errors link economic transport to full-policy loss. A constructive neural
8                backend retains the feasible actions attaining its labels and supports im-      8

9                plementation under acquired states and endogenous capacity. We give an          9

10               exact continuous-state compiler that preserves the original policy, includ-     10
                 ing ties, while removing a redundant state-cover factor from its evaluation
11                                                                                               11
                 work. The construction combines classical distance transforms with witness-
12                                                                                               12
                 preserving off-grid evaluation and numerical certification. A priced obser-
13               vation technology determines precision from a certified economic account.       13

14               Direct constrained-policy comparisons distinguish signed cost differences       14

15               from the ability to recoup a controller replacement fee. Matched services in-   15
                 clude uniform and error-driven conventional methods, failed refinements,
16                                                                                               16
                 and three- and four-dimensional nonlinear stress cases. The results separate
17                                                                                               17
                 representation savings, complete work, and economic decisions without
18               attributing a native-envelope capability exclusively to neural encoding.        18

19               Broader applications retain their own comparison conditions.                    19

20                                                                                               20

21                  K EYWORDS : Dynamic programming, Neural approximation, Policy cer-           21
                 tification, Recursive utility, Computational economics.

                                       1. INTRODUCTION                                        30

31       An economic decision uses a continuation value because its consequences extend       31

32   beyond the current period. A learned continuation is useful only to the extent           32
                                 NEURAL BELLMAN OPERATORS                                 3

1    that the decisions made with it are accurate. Prediction loss, a Bellman residual         1

2    at sampled states, and the value of the policy actually implemented are different         2

3    objects. Their distinction is particularly important when a counterfactual changes        3

4    future policies or when a recursive preference evaluates the continuation generated       4

5    by those policies rather than the optimum.                                                5

6      This paper develops Neural Bellman Operators (NBOs) as a policy-evaluation and          6

7    feasible-improvement method. An operator takes economic primitives, a current             7

8    policy, and computational resources as inputs. It returns a continuation, a feasible      8

9    policy, and an economic error account, or it records that the requested account was       9

10   not obtained within its cap. The continuation is evaluated against its own fitted         10

11   future. Current actions, policy-specific future values, numerical execution, and the      11

12   cost of verification enter the same account. The construction is not a proposal to        12

13   rename an arbitrary neural predictor as a Bellman operator.                               13

14     There are four parts to the argument. First, only action-dependent continuation         14

15   errors affect a current decision. Their oscillation, rather than their absolute level,    15

16   controls loss. A transport bound identifies when an anchor continuation remains           16

17   valid after the future economy changes. These results connect reuse and refresh           17

18   decisions to economic tolerances. They also preserve the original formulation in          18

19   controlled diffusions, recursive preferences, endogenous preferences, and games,          19

20   subject to the distinct hypotheses of those applications.                                 20

21     Second, a signed residual certificate controls a complete returned policy. Its upper    21

22   bound contains the widths of the residual enclosures, the suboptimality of the            22

23   selected actions, and the terminal enclosure. It is invariant to additive date-specific   23

24   shifts of the fitted continuation. This leads to a useful separation: a policy can be     24

25   accurate even though a coarse verifier does not establish its accuracy. We prove          25

26   that residual verification can be refined while retaining the original network, the       26

27   original deployed actor, and its original allowance. The resulting bound measures         27

28   improved knowledge of the same policy, not a policy improvement disguised as              28

29   additional verification.                                                                  29

30     Third, certification of each policy relative to the optimum does not rank two           30

31   policies. We construct their cost difference directly. Under expected-cost preferences,   31

32   an own-continuation Bellman telescoping identity produces an unbiased policy-cost         32
     4

1    score with a support width governed by the signed certificate. Coupling innovations      1

2    across policies and enclosing every simulated path gives simultaneous finite-sample      2

3    intervals for the difference in the original continuous-innovation economy. The          3

4    statistical assertion is conditional on frozen policies and the stated independent       4

5    simulation model; outward arithmetic and unresolved action-cell boundaries have          5

6    their own explicit treatment.                                                            6

7        Fourth, a deterministic neural construction connects primitive moduli to a com- 7
8    plete policy tolerance. A minimum of Lipschitz cones, realized exactly by ReLU           8

9    gates, fits each date’s own-future Bellman labels without inflating its Lipschitz        9

10   bound when those labels are inexact. The state, action, and arithmetic budgets are       10

11   specified before construction. This yields finite termination for a defined neural       11

12   backend rather than a claim about arbitrary nonlinear training. A new full matched       12

13   catalogue executes the same resolution ladder for this backend and a conventional        13

14   spline, including every unsuccessful rung and three isolated timing repetitions.         14

15       The action that attains a Bellman label supplies additional information. We retain   15

16   its index through the ReLU minimum circuit and deploy that action when its               16

17   cone is selected. A one-sided policy sandwich then avoids charging the separate          17

18   actor and critic accounts twice. Both the nearest-node neural ablation and the           18

19   spline receive a strengthened certificate. Their complete matched frontiers isolate      19

20   certificate sharpness from first-target work and actual policy cost.                     20

21       A feasible policy must also survive the information and arithmetic used to imple- 21
22   ment it. We give a uniform acquisition-aware theorem that permits discontinuous          22

23   witness selection, uncertain measured states and capacity-dependent repair. A cou- 23
24   pled two-state investment economy realizes all covering, integration and repair op- 24
25   erations explicitly, with a conventional fitted-value comparator. The original scalar    25

26   witness policies receive direct expected-cost comparisons at every common first          26

27   crossing, under a tolerance and simultaneous error account fixed before execution. 27
28   This establishes proximity within the declared decision band, not a signed neural        28

29   ranking. A catalogue-choice theorem makes the result usable when implementation          29

30   charges differ. The complete resource curves and an identical native-envelope con- 30
31   trol isolate what the construction, the certificate and the neural representation each   31

32   contribute.                                                                              32
                                  NEURAL BELLMAN OPERATORS                               5

1      Three further results connect this construction to computation and economic            1

2    implementation. First, a literal cone network repeats a scan over every state label      2

3    whenever a Bellman query evaluates its future continuation. On a tensor cover, we        3

4    compile this network once and answer an arbitrary continuous-state query from the        4

5    cell’s corner data. The compiler carries the original action witness and the original    5

6    tie rule. Consequently the improvement in evaluation work does not come from             6

7    changing the policy being evaluated. Classical separable distance transforms supply      7

8    the nodal computation; the additional policy argument establishes the continuous         8

9    off-grid identity and feasible witness realization. In the two-state construction this   9

10   removes the redundant factor that makes dense cone evaluation scale as N 6 rather        10

11   than N 4 . It does not remove the state cover or give a capability unavailable to the    11

12   identical native envelope.                                                               12

13     Second, we make observation accuracy a priced economic input. A finite-bit             13

14   sensor reports a state cell. Robust repair selects an action feasible throughout that    14

15   cell. The policy theorem then gives an augmented loss account consisting of the full- 15
16   information construction bound, an acquisition term decreasing geometrically in the      16

17   number of bits, and a linear information charge. Exact neighboring-bit comparisons       17

18   identify the precision minimizing this guarantee. This is an information-design          18

19   result for the stated technology, not an assertion that minimizing a bound also          19

20   minimizes the unknown actual loss.                                                       20

21     Third, we evaluate actual constrained-policy cost differences, independently of        21

22   their all-state regret certificates. The two installed controllers face common continu- 22
23   ous innovations and the same observation technology. A prospective replacement           23

24   charge turns simultaneous expected-cost intervals into a decision about whether          24

25   either replacement recoups its cost. That question differs both from the sign of the     25

26   policy difference and from exact equality. A complete matched construction study,        26

27   an error-driven conventional comparator, and higher-dimensional nonlinear stress         27

28   executions accompany the decision analysis. We charge compilation, adaptive pilots,      28

29   unsuccessful resolutions, repair and output, and report the joint policy-comparison      29

30   service separately. These distinctions permit a positive computational contribution      30

31   to the original NBO program without confusing a sharper certificate, a cheaper           31

32   representation, and a better economic policy.                                            32
     6

1        The numerical implementation starts from economic primitives. In the scalar             1

2    investment economy, it fits a fresh ReLU continuation backward against targets              2

3    using its own previously fitted future network. In the two-capital economy, it trains       3

4    hidden locations in a convex squared-ReLU continuation, with continuous simplex             4

5    actions and continuous uniform innovations. The second experiment includes                  5

6    a flexible nonnegative ridge dictionary as well as a quadratic projection. Each             6

7    comparator constructs its own future targets and uses the same final economic               7

8    criterion. Neither conventional values nor inherited neural weights are used as             8

9    labels for the neural services.                                                             9

10       We report the complete 150-service catalogue, not only successful candidates. In        10

11   the original capped scalar services the neural procedure reaches the all-state target       11

12   0.06 in all twelve cases and 0.04 in six, whereas the spline reaches both targets in        12

13   every case. In the coupled services the neural procedure reaches 0.25 in six cases          13

14   and 0.10 in five; the flexible ridge reaches both in all six. These statements describe     14

15   the original service, including its original verification cap.                              15

16       A subsequent diagnostic holds both the neural weights and the deployed policies         16

17   fixed. Refining only residual verification certifies all six previously unresolved scalar   17

18   policies at 0.04 and the previously unresolved coupled policy at 0.10. The original         18

19   failures are not relabeled. The diagnostic shows that they do not, by themselves,           19

20   identify a failure to construct an accurate neural policy. It does not identify the         20

21   population success probability of the optimizer, establish a new end-to-end runtime,        21

22   or change any policy’s cost.                                                                22

23       The comparative findings are equally integral to the paper. The original scalar         23

24   signed policy comparisons frequently favor the spline. The new coupled direct               24

25   intervals all contain zero and therefore do not order the policies. The precision           25

26   controller genuinely escalates after rejected low-precision proposals, but its com- 26
27   plete recorded work does not improve on fixed binary64 in this experiment. These            27

28   findings distinguish the certified capability of the construction from a comparative        28

29   advantage that would require additional evidence.                                           29

30       The paper retains its original economic subject and full theory. The exposition now     30

31   follows the economic problem, the operator, the policy theorem, and the evidence. 31
32   The complete previous article, proof supplement, and economic applications are              32
                                 NEURAL BELLMAN OPERATORS                                 7

1    materialized unchanged within the current source tree as preserved companions; the        1

2    preservation map records their exact identities. No historical theorem or application     2

3    is deleted to obtain a shorter active exposition. The present article and new technical   3

4    supplement state and prove the additional results used by the new evidence.               4

5                                                                                              5

6                                                                                              6

7                                                                                              7
                                  1.1. Relation to the literature
8                                                                                              8

9      Neural Bellman Operators sit within approximate dynamic programming, fitted- 9
10   value methods, and policy iteration, not outside them. Regression-based contin- 10
11   uation methods and simulation-based dynamic programming supply important                  11

12   conventional benchmarks (Judd, 1998, Powell, 2011, Longstaff and Schwartz, 2001,          12

13   Ernst et al., 2005). Actor–critic and approximate policy-iteration methods separate       13

14   evaluation and improvement (Konda and Tsitsiklis, 1999, Scherrer et al., 2015); safe- 14
15   improvement arguments likewise distinguish a proposed action from a verified              15

16   gain (Kakade and Langford, 2002, Pirotta et al., 2013). We use these distinctions to      16

17   organize the economic object returned by the algorithm.                                   17

18     Neural methods for differential equations provide ways to approximate values            18

19   and their derivatives (Sirignano and Spiliopoulos, 2018, Han et al., 2018, Hornik         19

20   et al., 1990). Such approximation does not on its own verify a returned dynamic           20

21   policy or its numerical execution. Our certificates can be applied to neural or           21

22   conventional continuations. The neural-specific construction lies in fresh own-future     22

23   training and, in the coupled experiment, learned hidden locations. Its comparative        23

24   value must be isolated against flexible conventional methods, not inferred from the       24

25   certificate’s validity.                                                                   25

26     The general economic formulation admits recursive utility (Epstein and Zin, 1989,       26

27   Duffie and Epstein, 1992). The compact-domain policy theorem uses monotonicity            27

28   and cash invariance, while the original Gaussian entropic construction has separate       28

29   moment and spectral conditions. These are different bridges, not interchangeable          29

30   assumptions. The direct paired-cost identity below applies to conditional expec- 30
31   tation; it is not asserted for a nonlinear certainty equivalent. The confidence step      31

32   uses the empirical Bernstein inequality of Maurer and Pontil (2009). Our additional       32
     8

1    ingredients are the policy-specific telescoping score, its certificate-derived support,          1

2    and the numerical enclosure of the original continuous-law comparison.                           2

3                                                                                                     3

15   The value is V a (t, s) = Yta under the well-posedness and comparison hypotheses                 15

16   in the retained controlled-economy analysis. In particular, the recursive argument               16

17   is the policy’s own continuation. Replacing it by the optimal continuation would                 17

18   change the policy-evaluation problem.                                                            18

19        The applications companion retains the full boundary specifications, infinite- 19
20   horizon transversality conditions, utility-domain restrictions, temporal-self equa- 20
21   tions, and game conditions. A finite-domain residual calculation does not establish              21

22   any of these by itself. In a game, certifying a specified deviation problem is not the           22

23   same as establishing the equilibrium fixed point. In continuous time, the controlled             23

24   generator contains state derivatives; freezing a critic during an actor update does              24

25   not suppress legitimate derivatives of the represented state-dependent function.                 25

26        For the constructive implementation and the main policy theorem, consider dates             26

27   t = 0, . . . , T, compact state space X, and feasible correspondence At ( x ). States evolve     27

                                  2.2. An executable NBO service
18                                                                                               18

19
       An implemented operator maps economic data and a current policy to                        19

20                                                                                               20

21                            b C, W)
                        ( fb, π,         or     (unresolved, W, diagnostic),                     21

22                                                                                               22

23   where C is an economic certificate and W records the work used. Its stages are target       23

24   construction, fitting, feasible action selection, independent certification, and durable    24

25   storage. Critic fitting freezes its future inputs; action selection freezes the resulting   25

26   critic. In the fresh backward construction, date t uses the already fitted fbt+1 , not an   26

27   externally solved optimal continuation. The terminal target is the primitive g.             27

28     A finite optimizer schedule is an algorithm, not a convergence theorem. A service         28

29   that has not met its target at its cap records failure, with the complete cost of           29

30   its attempts. The retained factor-training analysis provides a finite constructive          30

31   theorem for a separate structured class. The nonlinear schedules below instead have         31

32   an explicit, auditable capped-service estimand.                                             32

29     The proof compares the true and fitted objective at the two selected actions and                 29

30   couples the future states. The weighted finite-menu bound is Cauchy–Schwarz                        30

31   applied to ei − e j . Details are in the technical supplement. An additive continuation            31

1    does not certify a continuous action set merely because it includes both observed                      1

2    actions.                                                                                               2

3      For recursive maps with sup-norm Lipschitz constants β k on a common invariant                       3

4    utility domain, a one-step perturbation νk propagates as bk ≤ νk + β k bk+1 . This is                  4

5    the recursive counterpart of (6). Its use requires the common domain; it cannot cross                  5

6    a singular utility boundary by extending a local constant. These transport bounds                      6

7    retain the original refresh logic: use an anchor while its economic allowance covers                   7

8    approximation and future change, and recertify or recompute when that condition                        8

9    is exhausted.                                                                                          9

1         P ROOF : Monotonicity and cash invariance imply Tt (v + c) = Tt v + β t c and the            1

2    analogous identity for the fixed-policy map. Starting from g ≥ f T − u T , backward               2

3    induction gives the first inequality in (9). Starting from g ≤ f T − ℓ T and using                3

4    Qt ( f t+1 ; x, πt ( x )) ≤ f t ( x ) − ℓt + ηt gives the second. Subtraction proves the upper    4

       The theorem certifies a full adapted policy, not a finite action menu or a grid of              8

9
     initial states. With expected cost its proof is a Bellman telescoping argument; for a             9

10
     monotone cash-invariant recursive evaluation it is an order argument. The numeri- 10
11
     cal experiment is not needed for its validity. Its numerical usefulness depends on                11

12
     producing small, valid enclosures.                                                                12

22     The original actor allowance is valid on the entire state domain, so a finer critic             22

23   verification mesh does not invalidate it. Conversely, replacing it by an allowance for            23

24   a newly optimized fine-grid actor would certify a different policy. The implemen- 24
25   tation records the original actor hash and explicitly discards any fine-grid action               25

26   proposals generated internally by the verifier. Those proposals’ work and transient               26

27   storage are still charged.                                                                        27

28     The corollary does not promise that arbitrary precision is attainable by mesh                   28

29   refinement. Nodal residual variation, the retained actor allowance, and the terminal              29

30   enclosure can leave a positive floor. Nor is the corollary a claim that additional                30

31   verification improves welfare. It distinguishes the accuracy of a fixed economic                  31

32   decision rule from the sharpness of our knowledge of that accuracy.                               32
                                 NEURAL BELLMAN OPERATORS                                 13

1                      3.3. Construction beyond post-training verification                      1

2                                                                                               2
       The original trainable-factor construction remains a constructive part of NBO.
3                                                                                               3
     For a quadratic own-policy continuation x ′ Pt x + ct , a square-activation network
4                                                                                               4
     ∥Wt x ∥2 /d + cbt trains every entry of Wt . Its population derivative loss and gradient
5                                                                                               5
     are
6                                                                                               6

7                 Lt (W; P) = 14 ∥W ′ W − dP∥2F ,                     ′
                                                      ∇W Lt = W (W W − dP).                     7

8                                                                                               8
     If σmin (W ) ≥ s > 0, then ∥W ′ W/d − P∥ F ≤ ∥∇W Lt ∥ F /(ds). Together with the full-
9                                                                                               9
     vector actor defect, this gives a policy-error account for a jointly trained hidden
10                                                                                              10
     factor. The own-policy coefficient is re-evaluated for the returned policy, not silently
11                                                                                              11
     borrowed from an earlier training policy.
12                                                                                              12
       The primitive-budget analysis in the retained article chooses a feasibility witness,
13                                                                                              13
     coefficient envelopes, rank margins, and update budgets before training. In the
14                                                                                              14
     Gaussian entropic extension it also verifies positive exponential-moment margins.
15                                                                                              15
     Under its stated spectral hypotheses, a scaled orthogonal initialization and bounded
16                                                                                              16
     gradient steps give the finite training construction proved there. The finite matrix
17                                                                                              17
     and Gaussian formulas are not a nonlinear dimension-scaling experiment. They
18                                                                                              18
     also do not imply that Adam or L-BFGS has a deterministic residual cap for a
19                                                                                              19
     general squared-ReLU network. We preserve both results and keep the two logical
20                                                                                              20
     statements separate.

22                4. FROM ECONOMIC PRIMITIVES TO NONLINEAR CERTIFICATES                         22

23                                                                                              23
                            4.1. Backward fitting and selected policies
24                                                                                              24

25     For each method, initialize a fresh terminal fit from g. At date t, construct tar- 25
26   gets from Tt f t+1 using that method’s own already fitted future. Fit the current          26

27   continuation under the frozen schedule. Then propose feasible actions, enclose             27

28   the Bellman infimum and the selected-action gap, and validate residual coverage. 28
29   Store a checkpoint before reporting its first target crossing. Continue through the        29

30   declared ladder only as specified by the original study. The record includes all           30

31   attempted resolutions, unsuccessful target queries, target-generation work, and            31

32   storage operations.                                                                        32
     14

1      The scalar network has 63 ReLU features. Its training uses 513 states, a 257- 1
2    point target action construction, 400 Adam steps at learning rate 5 × 10−5 , and a         2

3    final output fit. The scalar verifier uses its own finer action and state ladders. The     3

4    conventional spline is reconstructed from the same primitives with its own ladder          4

5    and targets. A conventional target solver is not used to label a neural continuation. 5
6      The coupled neural continuation is                                                       6

     Hidden locations and weights are trained. Nonnegative coefficient initialization and       11

     a finite L-BFGS schedule of 300 iterations act on fresh own-future targets. The ridge      12

13
     comparator instead fixes a 96-feature squared-hinge dictionary and fits nonnegative        13

18                                                                                              18

19                         4.2. All-state and continuous-action verification                    19

20                                                                                              20
       At each state node, outward arithmetic encloses the actual network value and
21                                                                                              21
     the action objective. In the coupled economy, convexity in actions and a positive
22                                                                                              22
     strong-convexity constant yield a continuous-simplex primal–dual gap. A projected
23                                                                                              23
     optimizer merely proposes the action; its stopping flag is not the certificate. State
24                                                                                              24
     interpolation then extends the signed residual bounds off-grid. The nearest-node
25                                                                                              25
     deployed actor receives a separate, uniform allowance for using its node action at
26                                                                                              26
     the actual state.
27                                                                                              27
       Uniform innovations are integrated through positive-part moments, rather than
28                                                                                              28
     replaced by a sampled innovation law. The elementary identity for a uniform
29                                                                                              29
     variable U on [−h, h] is
30                                                                                              30

31                                                                                              31
                                        (y + h)3+ − (y − h)3+
32                        E(y + U )2+ =                       ,               h > 0.            32
                                                         6h
                                 NEURAL BELLMAN OPERATORS                                  15

1    Successive integration gives the corresponding formula for independent coordi- 1
2    nates and affine neuron arguments; zero coefficients are handled by the lower- 2
3    dimensional formula. Interval evaluation encloses cancellation and rounding. The            3

4    supplement specifies the mesh and action bounds used in the inherited verifier.             4

5      The residual account distinguishes a nodal enclosure from state coverage, actor           5

6    error, and terminal error. A nodal enclosure contains the effects of representation,        6

7    fitting, Bellman optimization, and arithmetic; it is not an identified decomposition        7

8    into a pure approximation error and a pure optimizer error. In particular, sub- 8
9    tracting a training loss from the residual does not estimate either component. The          9

10   diagnostic reports every separately available allowance without counting nested             10

11   subcomponents twice.                                                                        11

12                                                                                               12

13                            4.3. Work, dimension, and execution                                13

14                                                                                               14
       For tensor state, action, and innovation covers of sizes Kx , Ka , m, a direct verifier
15                                                                                               15
     pays work of order
16                                                                                               16

17                   ∑ Kx Ka {m(CF + Pt+1 ) + Cρ } + ∑ Kx Pt + Wstorage,                  (12)   17
                     t< T                               t≤ T
18                                                                                               18

19   where Pt counts network evaluation work. Actor storage is of order TKx d a . Analytic       19

20   integration replaces m by the work of the moment formula, not by zero. With                 20

21   tensor covers, Kx scales as h− dx
                                  x ; analogous factors arise for action and innovation          21

22   discretizations. A dimension-explicit certificate is therefore not a dimension-free         22

23   algorithm.                                                                                  23

24     If a Lipschitz innovation approximation contributes at most ζ t to Qt , widening          24

25   residual endpoints by ζ t and the actor allowance by 2ζ t adds 4 ∑t Bt ζ t to (8). This     25

26   is a discretization account, not permission to replace a continuous law without             26

27   charging its error. Independent policy queries may have their own scenario-tree or          27

28   simulation cost and are recorded separately.                                                28

29     The complete service includes own-future targets, fitting, unsuccessful refine- 29
30   ment, action extraction, verification, applicable policy queries, serialization, and        30

31   durable checkpoint storage. Initialization and outer-process time are retained sepa- 31
32   rately. New local diagnostic clocks are not added to old GitHub-hosted clocks to            32
     16

1    manufacture an end-to-end estimate. All reported arithmetic certificates concern                                     1

2    stored binary coefficients with outward enclosures. The inherited execution account                                  2

3    treats rounded state acquisition and transition/cost evaluation separately from                                      3

4    ideal policy loss.                                                                                                   4

                   6. INVESTMENT ECONOMIES AND THE COMPLETE EVIDENCE
28                                                                                           28
                                     6.1. Design and provenance
29                                                                                           29

30     The original science design has twelve scalar neural services, twelve scalar          30

31   spline services, six coupled neural services, six coupled ridge services, six coupled   31

32   quadratic services, and 36 services for each of three precision rules. The successful   32
     18

1    source-bound execution contains all 150 records. The original first run, with four              1

2    nonfinite-network exceptions, is retained. Its numerical inverse-softplus initializa- 2
3    tion was subsequently replaced by an algebraically equivalent stable expression;                3

4    economic primitives, seeds, targets, budgets, and comparators were unchanged. 4
5    We do not treat the failed first run as nonexistent or combine its clocks with the              5

6    successful run.                                                                                 6

7      The R44 protocol was committed before the additional diagnostics were executed. 7
8    It is a post-review protocol, not an original preregistration. It selects the seven             8

9    unresolved tight-target neural objects by their already observed outcomes and                   9

10   freezes their weights and policies. The paired comparison uses all six coupled pairs            10

11   and all four originally declared states. A source manifest binds the original inputs,           11

12   the diagnostic code, outcomes, and publication sources. Every original record and               12

13   all five original science-source hashes were replayed.                                          13

                                  6.7. What the evidence identifies                              30

31     The evidence identifies a finite from-primitives nonlinear construction, all-state        31

32   certificates for its returned policies, a useful retained-policy verification refinement,   32
                                NEURAL BELLMAN OPERATORS                              23

1    and direct economic comparisons under the declared state and innovation design. 1
2    It also identifies the continued strength of conventional comparators. It does not     2

3    identify a general neural speed advantage, a nonlinear high-dimensional frontier,      3

4    or a population probability of optimizer success.                                      4

5      An adaptive sparse-grid or adaptive-partition comparator has not been exe- 5
6    cuted for the original neural optimizers. Section 15 adds a separate error-driven      6

7    coordinate-FVI comparator and three- and four-dimensional stress catalogue for         7

8    the constructive backend; neither retroactively changes the optimizer study. Neither   8

9    has a nonlinear dimension frontier or repeated isolated timing of every original       9

10   scalar and coupled service. Section 7.3 adds a separate predeclared horizon/price      10

11   catalogue with repeated isolated timing for the deterministic constructive backend     11

12   and spline; it does not replace the original experiments. The retained dimension- 12
13   explicit analysis exposes the tensor factors those studies would have to confront,     13

14   rather than treating a two-state experiment as their substitute. The general eco- 14
15   nomic framework and conditional constructive results remain in force; the missing      15

16   experiments concern comparative evidence for broader implementation claims.            16

17                                                                                          17

18          7. A CONSTRUCTIVE NEURAL SERVICE WITH A PRIMITIVE ERROR BUDGET                  18

19
       The policy theorem is deliberately independent of the representation of the con- 19
20                                                                                          20
     tinuation. A construction theorem must do more: it must produce a representation,
21                                                                                          21
     feasible actions, and verified residuals from economic primitives. This section
22                                                                                          22
     supplies such a theorem for a deterministic ReLU backend. It does not assume
23
     convergence of the nonlinear optimizers used in the earlier experiments. Its distin- 23
24                                                                                          24
     guishing property is that the continuation has a prescribed Lipschitz constant even
25                                                                                          25
     when its Bellman labels are inexact.
26                                                                                          26

27                                                                                          27
                      7.1. Primitive conditions and the neural architecture
28                                                                                          28

29     Consider the compact Bellman economy of Section 2, with a common action set          29

30   A ⊂ Ra and state set X ⊂ Rd . All distances in this section use the ℓ1 norm. The       30

31   innovation law and the monotone, cash-invariant functional ρt do not depend            31

32   on the state or action. The transition remains in X. Suppose known nonnegative         32
     24

1    constants satisfy, uniformly in the innovation,                                                            1

2                                                                                                               2

3                             |ct ( x, a) − ct (y, b)| ≤ Cx,t ∥ x − y∥1 + Ca,t ∥ a − b∥1 ,               (17)   3

4                    ∥ Ft ( x, a, z) − Ft (y, b, z)∥1 ≤ Fx,t ∥ x − y∥1 + Fa,t ∥ a − b∥1 .                (18)   4

5                                                                                                               5

6    The terminal cost is L g -Lipschitz. These assumptions are additional conditions                           6

7    on the constructive backend, not a replacement for the more general feasible cor- 7
8    respondences in the original economic formulation. A state-dependent feasible                              8

9    correspondence requires its own feasible-cover and transport constants.                                    9

10     We require an effective numerical description of the primitives: certified finite                        10

11   state and action covers can be obtained at every positive radius; their nodes are                          11

12   feasible; and a numerical evaluator can enclose each Bellman query to any requested                        12

13   positive tolerance in finite work. The last condition includes evaluation of ρt , not                      13

14   just evaluation of the network. For expectation with a compact innovation domain,                          14

15   known cell probabilities and a certified innovation cover provide one such evaluator. 15
16   If Ft is Fz,t -Lipschitz in the innovation, replacing each cell by a representative at                     16

17   distance at most hz contributes at most β t Lt+1 Fz,t hz . Arithmetic, probability-mass                    17

18   approximation, and any remaining integration error must be added. The experiment                           18

19   below instead integrates a piecewise-linear network analytically under the original                        19

20   continuous uniform law.                                                                                    20

21     Set                                                                                                      21

22                                                                                                              22
                  LT = Lg ,       Lt = Cx,t + β t Fx,t Lt+1 ,             Lta = Ca,t + β t Fa,t Lt+1 .   (19)
23                                                                                                              23

24   Choose state nodes xt,i of covering radius ht and action nodes at,j of covering radius                     24

25   k t . Given the continuation already constructed at date t + 1, compute numbers qbt,ij                     25

26   satisfying                                                                                                 26

                                      |qbt,ij − Qt ( f t+1 ; xt,i , at,j )| ≤ et .                       (20)
28                                                                                                              28

29   Define yt,i = min j qbt,ij , choose a minimizing action index j(t, i ) with a fixed tie rule,              29

                                    f t ( x ) = min{yt,i + Lt ∥ x − xt,i ∥1 }.                           (21)   32

                                       NEURAL BELLMAN OPERATORS                                     25

1    At date T, use labels within eT of g and the same formula with constant L g . At any                 1

2    other date, deploy the action at,j(t,i( x)) attached to a nearest state node, again with             2

3    a fixed tie rule. The action is feasible by construction. No future optimal value or                 3

4    conventional comparator is used as a label.                                                          4

5      Formula (21) is an exact ReLU construction, not an appeal to a universal approxi- 5
6    mation theorem. Absolute values use |u| = max{u, 0} + max{−u, 0}; minima use                         6

7    min{u, v} = u − max{u − v, 0}. A balanced tree of these gates implements the finite                  7

8    minimum. With Kt state nodes, the resulting sparse affine–ReLU circuit has O(dKt )                   8

9    coefficients and O(1 + log Kt ) depth with unrestricted affine fan-in. Signed interme- 9
10   diate values can be carried by their positive and negative parts. The construction is                10

11   related to the classical Lipschitz extension formula of McShane (1934); neither that                 11

12   formula nor its elementary ReLU realization is claimed as new. Here its role is to                   12

13   close an own-future dynamic construction, including the deployed actor and every                     13

14   numerical allowance.                                                                                 14

15                                                                                                        15

16
       T HEOREM 5—Constructive own-future neural policy: Under the preceding condi- 16
17
     tions, the network (21) is Lt -Lipschitz at every date, independently of the numerical label         17

18
     errors. Its residual and its deployed actor satisfy                                                  18

                                                  −et ≤ f t − Tt f t+1 ≤ Lta k t + et + 2Lt ht ,   (22)
20                                                                                                        20

21
                Qt ( f t+1 ; x, πt ( x )) − Tt f t+1 ( x ) ≤ Lta k t + 2et + 2Lt ht .              (23)   21

22                                                                                                        22
     At the terminal date, −eT ≤ f T − g ≤ eT + 2L g h T . Consequently the cost of the returned
23                                                                                                        23
     policy, relative to the optimum over all admissible adapted policies, is uniformly bounded by
24                                                                                                        24

     For fixed nodes and Lt , changing every label by at most δ changes the represented function
28                                                                                                        28
     by at most δ in the uniform norm; it does not change the Lipschitz bound.
29                                                                                                        29

30     P ROOF : Monotonicity and cash invariance imply nonexpansiveness in the                            30

31   uniform norm. Thus Qt ( f t+1 ; ·, a) and Tt f t+1 are Lt -Lipschitz, while the action               31

32   modulus is Lta . At a node, minimization of the numerical queries gives −et ≤                        32
     26

1    yt,i − Tt f t+1 ( xt,i ) ≤ Lta k t + et . Each cone in (21) is Lt -Lipschitz, as is their finite min- 1
2    imum. The lower residual bound follows from the triangle inequality for every                         2

3    cone; the upper bound uses a nearest node twice, once for the cone and once                           3

4    for the Bellman value. At that node the selected action is within Lta k t + 2et of the                4

5    continuous-action infimum. Transport to x costs at most 2Lt ht . The terminal argu- 5
6    ment is identical without action minimization. Substitution of these widths and                       6

7    actor allowances into Theorem 2 gives (24). Finally, minima of two arrays whose                       7

8    entries differ by at most δ differ by at most δ. The supplement gives the construction,               8

9    measurability, and work details.                                                           Q.E.D.     9

10                                                                                                         10
          C OROLLARY 6—Deterministic termination at an economic tolerance: Let ε > 0,
11                                                                                                         11
     H = ∑tT=0 Bt , and s = ε/H. At nonterminal dates choose
12                                                                                                         12

13                           et ≤ s/8,        Lta k t ≤ s/8,     Lt ht ≤ s/16.                      (25)   13

14                                                                                                         14
     At the terminal date choose eT ≤ s/4 and L g h T ≤ s/4. Terms with zero coefficient impose
15                                                                                                         15
     no restriction. Then the finite constructive service returns a feasible neural policy with loss
16                                                                                                         16
     at most ε. No probability law over initializations and no optimizer stopping criterion is
17                                                                                                         17
     needed.
18                                                                                                         18

19     The result follows because each discounted bracket in (24) is at most s. Effec- 19
20   tive compactness and the numerical evaluator give finite work for these choices. 20
21   Arbitrarily small tolerances may require arbitrary precision; the corollary does                      21

22   not promise termination in fixed binary64. A finite implementation ladder can                         22

23   still return a recorded failure, even though the unbounded constructive algorithm                     23

24   terminates under the stated hypotheses.                                                               24

25     The earlier scalar constructive result in the retained paper implemented piecewise- 25
26   linear interpolation as a ReLU network. Its sufficient allocation controlled a label- 26
27   error-to-mesh ratio in the propagated slope. Formula (21) works in arbitrary finite                   27

28   state dimension and enforces the slope bound directly. In particular, label and                       28

29   action allowances can be allocated at order ε without a separate slope-preservation                   29

30   requirement that those allowances be quadratic in the state spacing. This is an                       30

31   improvement of that sufficient proof budget, not a lower bound for all spline                         31

32   methods or evidence that conventional Lipschitz projection is unavailable.                            32
                                     NEURAL BELLMAN OPERATORS                           27

1                                   7.2. What the construction costs                          1

2                                                                                             2
       The construction does not eliminate state coverage. With Kt state nodes, Jt action
3                                                                                             3
     nodes, Mt innovation representatives, and network evaluation cost Pt+1 , direct
4                                                                                             4
     target formation requires
5                                                                                  !          5
                                                                               T
6
                     ∑ Kt Jt { Mt (CF,t + Pt+1 ) + Cρ,t + Cc,t } + O ∑ dKt             (26)   6

7                   t< T                                                   t =0               7

8    arithmetic operations under the indicated evaluation model. Cρ,t includes the finite- 8
9    risk functional and probability processing. Certified integration can replace the        9

10   innovation sum but its own cost must be charged. Bit lengths, adaptive arithmetic,       10

11   and nearest-node policy queries are additional explicit costs, not constants sup- 11
12   pressed by a dimension claim. A rectangular state cover with radius ht can require       12

13   order h− d                                                     −a
            t nodes; a rectangular action cover can require order k t actions. The theo-
                                                                                         13

14   rem is therefore a dimension-explicit construction, not an assertion that a nonlinear    14

15   high-dimensional frontier has been executed or that the curse of dimensionality has      15

16   been removed.                                                                            16

17     In one state dimension, the finite envelope can be compiled exactly into a             17

18   piecewise-linear representation. Two passes compute the greatest L-Lipschitz mino- 18
19   rant of the node labels. Between neighboring nodes the envelope is the minimum           19

20   of the two adjacent affine cones. At most one additional intersection is needed per      20

21   interval. Rational knots and values permit exact antiderivatives; an interval evalu- 21
22   ator encloses their numerical evaluation. This compilation evaluates the defining        22

23   network exactly and does not substitute labels from a separately solved Bellman          23

24   problem. The spline comparator receives the same exact-integration acceleration, so      24

25   it is not charged for an artificial dense neural evaluation loop.                        25

27                           7.3. A predeclared matched service catalogue                     27

28                                                                                            28
       The new experiment tests complete services, not only previously failed neural
29                                                                                            29
     candidates. It uses a further nonlinear investment laboratory with x ∈ [0, 1], a ∈
30                                                                                            30
     [0, 1/4], β = 15/16, and independent z uniform on [−1/32, 1/32]:
31                                                                                            31
                                          1
32                         F ( x, a, z) = 32 + 11     1
                                               16 x + 16 x (1 − x ) + a + z,           (27)   32
     28
                                                  2    2    4            2
1                         c p ( x, a) = ( x − 11                  3
                                              16 ) + pa + 4a + 2( 8 − x )+ ,             (28)   1

2                                             11 2                                              2
                              g( x ) = 2( x − 16 ) + 2( 38 − x )2+ .                     (29)
3                                                                                               3

4    The invariant domain follows from monotonicity in x, a, z and the two extreme              4

5    transitions 0 and 1. Valid primitive state moduli are Cx = 23/8, Fx = 3/4, and             5

6    L g = 17/4; action moduli are Ca = p/2 + 1/4 and Fa = 1. The downside term                 6

7    penalizes a low capital stock, and the quartic investment cost discourages rapid           7

8    adjustment. This is a numerical economic laboratory, not an estimated or calibrated        8

9    quantitative exercise.                                                                     9

10     The complete design was fixed before execution: horizons {2, 4}, prices {1, 4},          10

11   two methods, three isolated repetitions, and the full common state/action ladder           11

12   N = A ∈ {16, 32, 64, 128, 256, 512}. The targets are 0.5, 0.25, and 0.125 in units of      12

13   discounted cost. These are policy-loss tolerances, not equivalence margins for a           13

14   neural-minus-spline contrast. Every rung restarts from terminal primitives and             14

15   constructs its own future backward. The neural method uses (21); the spline uses           15

16   linear interpolation of its own labels and propagates its actual exact slope bound. 16
17   Both evaluate the same continuous innovation law analytically and round labels to          17

18   dyadic multiples of 2−40 . All numerical query errors are outwardly enclosed and           18

19   enter the certificate.                                                                     19

20     All rungs are retained, including those preceding the first target crossing. The         20

21   clock to a target includes every preceding rung, target formation, rational com- 21
22   pilation, actor construction, verification, and durable checkpoint output. Fresh           22

23   processes run sequentially on one selected CPU with one numerical-library thread           23

24   and an identical non-economic warm-up. Imports and warm-up are excluded from               24

25   the internal clock but included in a separate process clock. CPU frequency is not          25

26   fixed. Independent policy-cost comparison is not performed in this catalogue and           26

27   is not assigned a zero cost. Three timing repetitions describe local dispersion, not a     27

28   population law for neural optimization.                                                    28

29     The neural backend certifies all twelve services at 0.5 and 0.25, and nine of twelve     29

30   at 0.125. The spline certifies all twelve at every target. The three neural failures are   30

31   repetitions of the same horizon-four, price-four economy: the final gap is approxi- 31
32   mately 0.12503, just above 0.125. They remain failures rather than being rounded           32

                       8. NEURAL CONTINUATION WITH AN ACTION WITNESS                                   9

10     A constructed continuation contains information about the action that produced                  10

11   each Bellman label. Discarding that information and subsequently controlling an                   11

12   unrelated actor can charge the same approximation twice. We now retain the label’s                12

13   feasible action through the minimum circuit. This changes the returned policy, not                13

14   merely the description of an old certificate. The construction continues to use the               14

15   original economic primitives and its own fitted future.                                           15

16                                                                                                     16

17                                                                                                     17
                                       8.1. A one-sided policy sandwich
18                                                                                                     18

       Write Ttπ f t+1 ( x ) = Qt ( f t+1 ; x, πt ( x )). Suppose that, uniformly on the invariant     19

20
     domain,                                                                                           20

     The quantities ut and dt are signed upper bounds. Their separate absolute values
24                                                                                                     24
     need not enter a policy-loss account.

16   A fixed tie convention makes this finite selector measurable. The common action set                          16

17   makes each selected action feasible, even when the attaining cone is not centered at a                       17

18   nearest node. The critic remains the exact affine–ReLU circuit. The actor additionally                       18

19   uses the circuit’s comparison index; a discontinuous selector is not asserted to be a                        19

20   continuous ReLU output.                                                                                      20

1                          8.3. A strengthened conventional comparison                           1

2                                                                                                2
       For either the original nearest-node cone actor or the spline actor, let Ci be the
3
     node’s closed nearest-neighbor cell and let f t be that method’s own continuation. 3
4                                                                                                4
     The same selected-label argument gives the computable bound
5                                                                                                5

6               d t = e t + Dt ,    Dt = max sup {yt,i + Lt ∥ x − xt,i ∥1 − f t ( x )}.   (35)   6
                                            i   x ∈Ci
7                                                                                                7

8    On the scalar domain, all terms are piecewise affine. Their maximum is attained             8

9    among the continuation knots, state nodes and cell boundaries; both adjacent actors         9

10   are included at a boundary. The exact rational maximum supplies a valid all-state           10

11   allowance without an additional verification mesh. Both conventional interpolation          11

12   and the nearest-node neural ablation receive this improved account. Thus the                12

13   experiment does not contrast the new bound only against a deliberately loose                13

14   baseline. The old separated bound remains recorded, and the smaller of two bounds           14

15   for the same returned policy may be used.                                                   15

                    8.4. Matched construction, policy and certificate frontiers                  17

18     The following matched catalogue is the frozen R46 experiment. Its statements              18

19   about direct policy cost refer to the evidence then available; Section 13 supplies          19

20   the new actual-policy comparison, and Section 12 executes the state-dependent               20

21   construction. The design was fixed in the repository before execution. It retains           21

22   the nonlinear continuous-innovation economy (27), horizons 2, 4, prices 1, 4, and           22

23   all six resolutions 16, 32, 64, 128, 256, 512. Three methods and three isolated timing      23

24   repetitions produce 36 services and 216 rungs. Each rung restarts from primitives. 24
25   The two cone methods have identical critics and node-action arrays; their deployed          25

26   selectors differ. The spline constructs its own labels. All methods receive the same        26

27   exact integration and outward numerical arithmetic.                                         27

28     The declared targets are 1/4, 1/8 and 1/16. Every unsuccessful rung and every             28

29   capped failure is retained. Internal prefix clocks include target construction, witness     29

30   or nearest-cell compilation, certificates and durable checkpoints; process clocks           30

31   additionally include imports and the common non-economic warm-up. Runs use                  31

32   one selected CPU and one numerical-library thread, with method order rotated                32
     34

1    across repetitions and economic cells. Frequency is not controlled. These are local                    1

2    measurements, not a hardware-independent work theorem. Primitive counts and                            2

3    rational storage are recorded separately from clocks, and are not called complete                      3

4    floating-point or bit-operation counts.                                                                4

17     All three methods certify 12/12 services at 1/4 and at 1/8, and 6/12 at 1/16. The                    17

18   latter failures are all horizon-four services and are retained. The witness bound                      18

19   is strictly tighter than the nearest-node neural ablation in all 24 distinct econ- 19
20   omy/resolution comparisons, and tighter than the strengthened spline in 23 of 24. 20
21   The exception is T = 4, p = 1, N = 16, where the witness and spline bounds are                         21

22   approximately 2.06039 and 2.03515. The declared dyadic targets have the same first- 22
23   crossing resolution for all three methods in every economic cell. Thus the certificate                 23

24   improvement does not yield an earlier first crossing on this particular ladder. The                    24

25   spline is faster in all 30 common successful target/repetition clock comparisons; the                  25

26   witness construction is faster than the nearest-node neural ablation in all 30. These                  26

27   local clocks and tighter regret bounds establish neither policy-cost superiority nor                   27

28   a general neural speed advantage. The comparisons isolate the effect of carrying                       28

29   the action witness and of using a joint policy certificate. They do not reclassify                     29

30   earlier capped services or claim that the new selector was previously deployed. 30
31   In particular, an improvement shared by the strengthened nearest-node ablation                         31

32   cannot be attributed solely to witness selection. A tighter regret certificate does not                32

1    identify the sign of a direct policy-cost contrast. The existing direct expected-cost          1

2    intervals and adverse precision findings remain unchanged.                                     2

       Investment opportunities often depend on current capital, collateral or remain- 5
6                                                                                                   6
     ing capacity. The common-action construction above does not by itself authorize
7                                                                                                   7

8                                                                                                   8

9                                                                                                   9
     controlled economy, rather than a relaxed control problem, remains the comparison
10                                                                                                  10
     object.

29      This result closes a constructive route for a class of endogenous constraints within                29

30   the original model. It does not assume that all feasible correspondences have a                        30

31   finite Hausdorff modulus. With s = ε/ ∑tT=0 Bt , sufficient nonterminal allocations                    31

32   are et ≤ s/8, DtQ k t ≤ s/4, Lt ht ≤ s/8 and DtQ ζ t ≤ s/4. Together with the earlier                  32
                                  NEURAL BELLMAN OPERATORS                                 37

1    terminal allocation, they imply finite termination at tolerance ε when all listed           1

2    operations are effective. Zero coefficients impose no restriction. When exact repair        2

3    is available, the larger query allowance of Corollary 9 is again sufficient. Setting        3

4    KtA = ζ t = 0 recovers the common-action witness theorem.                                   4

5                                                                                                5
       A capacity-constrained investment rule. For two nonnegative state coordinates
6                                                                                                6
     on [0, 1]2 , let A( x ) = [0, ( x1 + x2 )/2]. This is a state-dependent capital-capacity
7                                                                                                7
     constraint with K A = 1/2 in the ℓ1 state norm. The repair Γi ( x ) = min{ ai , ( x1 +
8                                                                                                8
     x2 )/2} is feasible, satisfies (37) with ζ = 0, and has an elementary affine–ReLU
9                                                                                                9
     representation for each fixed witness. Thus changing capacity changes the actor and
10                                                                                               10
     the constructive state modulus through an explicit term D Q /2. The index selector
11                                                                                               11
     remains a finite comparison procedure. The supplement provides exact rational
12                                                                                               12
     checks for this example; these are checks of the theorem and admissibility contract,
13                                                                                               13
     not an additional multidimensional performance study.
14                                                                                               14
       Neither feasible-net construction nor repair is free. With Jt,i node actions, target-
15                                                                                               15
     query work is summed over ∑i Jt,i rather than a common Kt Jt . Each deployed query
16                                                                                               16
     also incurs the selected repair’s work and storage. Under imperfect state acquisition,
17                                                                                               17
     repair must be certified feasible at the true state, or throughout the acquisition set
18                                                                                               18
     for a robust implementation. An error allowance for the objective cannot substitute
19                                                                                               19
     for that feasibility condition.

                        10. FROM ACQUIRED STATES TO FEASIBLE POLICIES
22                                                                                               22
       A policy is implemented at an acquired state, not at an exact real number available
23                                                                                               23
     without cost. This distinction matters for a witness selector: a small change in the
24                                                                                               24
     state can change its index discontinuously. The appropriate object is therefore an
25                                                                                               25
     objective allowance for the selected witness together with feasibility at the true state.
26                                                                                               26
     A Lipschitz assumption on the actor would replace, rather than solve, this problem.

28   ties. The supplement states the representability and arithmetic conditions and                               28

31     This implementation does not round a rational switchpoint and assume that its                              31

32   original cell is unchanged. For the scalar comparisons, all cells intersecting a state                       32

1    enclosure are retained. For acquired constrained deployment, a numerical index has               1

2    the objective allowance (46) and its action is repaired against the entire acquisition           2

3    set. Both routes address discontinuity directly.                                                 3

5                             10.2. The identical non-neural envelope                                 5

6                                                                                                     6
       The envelope construction must be separated from the language used to encode it.
7                                                                                                     7
     The classical Lipschitz extension (McShane, 1934), a native minimum computation,
8                                                                                                     8
     and an exact ReLU realization can represent the same function.
9                                                                                                     9

10                                                                                                    10
          P ROPOSITION 13—Representation control: Fix the node locations, labels, slopes,
11                                                                                                    11
     original action witnesses, and selector tie rule. A conventional min-plus implementation
12                                                                                                    12
     and the affine–ReLU implementation of the same envelope return the same mathematical
13                                                                                                    13
     continuation and policy. They have the same exact policy certificate and direct policy cost. A
14                                                                                                    14
     balanced realization uses O(dK ) arithmetic and comparison gates for K cones and depth
15                                                                                                    15
     O(log K + log d). Merely changing the representation does not improve the covering or
16                                                                                                    16
     Bellman-query factors.
17                                                                                                    17

18        P ROOF : For every pair of real numbers, min{ a, b} = a − ( a − b)+ . Applying              18

19   this identity through a balanced reduction tree proves equality of the continuation. 19
20   Carrying the same attaining index, with the prescribed tie rule, proves equality                 20

21   of the action witness and repaired action. The certificates and costs concern these              21

22   mathematical objects. The gate count includes the coordinate absolute values and                 22

23   their sums. The supplement separately bounds floating-point gate errors; exact                   23

24   equality is not asserted for unchecked rounded evaluations.                           Q.E.D.     24

25                                                                                                    25

26     We execute this non-neural control on all 24 distinct scalar witness checkpoints. 26
27   It shares the defining data rather than masquerading as an independently trained                 27

28   competitor. The native selector and its ReLU encoding are thus one construction                  28

29   with two representations. The learned hidden-location methods studied earlier                    29

30   are different candidate generators. Their assessment, and that of the deterministic              30

31   backend, remains a comparison of actual policies and complete computational                      31

32   work.                                                                                            32
                                     NEURAL BELLMAN OPERATORS                                       41

1              11. WITNESS-PRESERVING COMPILATION OF THE NEURAL OPERATOR                                  1
